Governo<sup>r</sup> and Company w<sup>ch</sup> shall be sould for their most aduantage, and yf they shall desyre the proceede thereof to be sent backe in the generall stocke, their request therein shall be ꝑformed, but hencforth noe priuate adventure to any ffacto<sup>r</sup> will be ꝑmitted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">And yf any of you dislike of the restrainte of yo<sup>r</sup> priuate trade, wee haue giuen order that yo<sup>u</sup> may retourne in theis Shipps: but yf you shall be willinge to stay, yo<sup>u</sup> may expect the Company will noe lesse Consider of yo<sup>r</sup> deserueinges then yo<sup>r</sup> care and diligence shall giue them iust occasion, for hitherto they haue never beene vngratefull vnto any that haue well deserued.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-403"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="403" data-folio="311" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n402_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 311">[p. 311]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">London the         of March 1609.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-121" href="#sec-07-fn-121" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-121" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">To m<sup>r</sup> Sharpey &amp; m<sup>r</sup> Rowles</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Forasmuch</span> as since yo<sup>r</sup> depture we haue receiued noe łres from yo<sup>u</sup>, nor M<sup>r</sup> Rowles and yett haue hard by łres written from Cairo home (thoughe vncertaine intelligence) that yo<sup>u</sup> haue had good traffique at Mocho both in sellinge o<sup>r</sup> English Comodities and byinge Indico and other m<sup>r</sup>chandize whereof we hope (though we haue not receiued them) yo<sup>u</sup> haue written łres by the Carivans and sent Iohn Iourden over land according to yo<sup>r</sup> Comission, w<sup>ch</sup> yf yo<sup>u</sup> haue not done will argue greate remisnes &amp; may not onlie greatelie hinder the Company, but alsoe discouradge such ffacto<sup>rs</sup> as yo<sup>u</sup> haue lefte in those parte, for want of supplies Wherefore we haue by S<sup>r</sup> Henry Middleton Generall of this o<sup>r</sup> sixt voyadge not onlie written theis few lines vnto yo<sup>u</sup> but alsoe by him sent łres vnto any such ffactory as yo<sup>u</sup> may in any place haue planted praying yf by the prouidence of God yo<sup>u</sup> shall meete together that vpon receipt of this o<sup>r</sup> łre yo<sup>u</sup> would relate vnto S<sup>r</sup> Hen: Middleton, [or] his viceadmirall one m<sup>r</sup> ffeemell the whole proceedinge of yo<sup>r</sup> voyadge and to aduise him where yo<sup>u</sup> haue traded, where yo<sup>u</sup> finde least danger, &amp; most apparance of proffitt, As alsoe yf yo<sup>u</sup> haue lefte any of o<sup>r</sup> stocke behinde, where, and at whose hande yt may be demanded to thend he may accordinge to order giuen him send or bringe home the same when he retourneth.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">ffurther we desier yo<sup>u</sup> to assist him and m<sup>r</sup> Lawrance ffeemell w<sup>th</sup> yo<sup>r</sup> best advice not onlie w<sup>ch</sup> of those ffacto<sup>rs</sup> yo<sup>r</sup> selfe haue placed in that Country, but alsoe w<sup>ch</sup> amongst all the ffacto<sup>rs</sup> that wee haue either at Aden, Suratt, Bantam, or ells where w<sup>th</sup> whome yo<sup>u</sup> may haue conversed are the fittest men to be ymployed in o<sup>r</sup> service both in reguard of their honest carriadge, and sufficient knowledge of that Country Comodities.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-404"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="404" data-folio="312" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n403_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 312">[p. 312]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1">London y<sup>e</sup> 19<sup>th</sup> of March. 1609.</h2><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Iohn Hearne Robert Browne Henry Sydall Bantam</aside></div><p class="prose-p">BY the Dragon w<sup>ch</sup> God be praysed arryved at Plymouth the middle of Septembƀ last we receiued lres twoe from yo<sup>u</sup> Iohn Hearne one from Roƀt Browne one from Iohn Sarys &amp; one from Henry Sydall: By Iohn Hearnes łre the third of Septembƀ we haue aduise what pepp, Alloes, white Benyamin and silke was sent for o<sup>r</sup> accompt all w<sup>ch</sup> we haue receiued by Godę favo<sup>r</sup> althoughe the Ship was very leakye and came weaklie home, in soe yll case as that we durst scarce adventure her (after she was lightened aboue 100 Tonns []) to come for London notw<sup>th</sup>standinge we finde avery greate want of the pepp yo<sup>u</sup> write was put abourd, to the full want as yt appeth to vs to be, Aboue twentie Tuns each Tun cont 2000 waight:<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-122" href="#sec-07-fn-122" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-122" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> w<sup>ch</sup> seemeth the more strange vnto vs in that the pepp yo<sup>u</sup> laded at Bantam had lyen soe longe and dryed soe much, that of 7489 sackę, there was lost 238 there, all w<sup>ch</sup> Considered together maketh vs thinke that o<sup>r</sup> busines was not well husbanded neither w<sup>th</sup> yo<sup>u</sup> nor in the Shipp comeinge home: Therefore we pray yo<sup>u</sup> that hereafter nothinge be bought nor sould privatelie but that yo<sup>r</sup> Iournall or booke Couchant may contynually lay open for eu<sup>r</sup>y one that is o<sup>r</sup> ffacto<sup>r</sup> to looke vpon, for wee hould yt noe way fitt, that he that is o<sup>r</sup> cheefe ffacto<sup>r</sup>, should carrie all the accomptę close to him selfe in such sorte that those whoe are w<sup>th</sup> him can giue noe reason howe o<sup>r</sup> Comodities are lessened, guiftę given, nor howe the wantę doe happen as some of yo<sup>r</sup> selues haue written vnto vs in theis yo<sup>r</sup> łres, that yo<sup>u</sup> can yeald vs noe reason w<sup>ch</sup> way the foresaid 238 baggę of pepp should be lost:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">We are sorry that there hath not beene a Contynuall booke kept there that we might thereby haue discerned in good sorte howe o<sup>r</sup> busines hath beene carried. ffor we are giuen to vnderstand that m<sup>r</sup> Scott brought away his owne accomptę as alsoe m<sup>r</sup> Towerson did the lyke and lefte noe Coppies of them w<sup>th</sup> yo<sup>u</sup>. It had beene fittinge for such as are the Companies servantę to haue kept a booke for the Company, there to haue remaned, and haue brought home a Coppy thereof for their owne vse, or at least so much thereof as might concerne them. ffor an amendm<sup>t</sup> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-405" data-page="405" data-folio="313" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n404_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 313">[p. 313]</button> whereof we haue sent yo<sup>u</sup> a lardge booke to be a Contynuall Couchant soe longe as yt shall last, willinge yo<sup>u</sup> the principall ffactor<sup>r</sup> and those that shall succeede yo<sup>u</sup>, that from hencforth yo<sup>u</sup> suffer those that are factors<sup>rs</sup> w<sup>th</sup> yo<sup>u</sup> to be priuie to all thinge bought and sould or otherwyse to be disposed of As alsoe that yo<sup>r</sup> booke and accompte may be publique and comon to all the rest of o<sup>r</sup> ffactors<sup>rs</sup>, w<sup>ch</sup> shall be good for yo<sup>r</sup> selfe to avoyed scandall or suspi<span class="sc">c</span>õn: And will be alsoe agood informa<span class="sc">c</span>õn vnto the rest of the ffactors<sup>rs</sup> vnder yo<sup>u</sup>, and bringe contentm<sup>t</sup> vnto vs, to free vs from suspi<span class="sc">c</span>õn of any practizes when yo<sup>u</sup> shall carry yo<sup>r</sup> busines publiquelie.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wee write vnto yo<sup>u</sup> twoe yeares past by the <em>Assencõn</em> and <em>Vnion</em> as alsoe by the <em>Expedicõn</em>, Captaine, David Middleton, the Coppies of w<sup>ch</sup> łres we doe herew<sup>th</sup>all send yo<sup>u</sup>.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-123" href="#sec-07-fn-123" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-123" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Albeyt we haue heretofore suffered yo<sup>u</sup> to haue private trade, yett the Company hath now vpon speciall considera<span class="sc">c</span>õn resolued, Not to pmitt yt any longer, for that we finde that those whoe haue had the manadgeinge of o<sup>r</sup> busines, haue had more care to inrich them selues, then to seeke o<sup>r</sup> proffitt, for yf they had not soe done they might haue ymployed o<sup>r</sup> stocke (w<sup>ch</sup> they haue had in their hande since S<sup>r</sup> Iames Lancaster came from thence) soe as the benefitt thereof might haue borne the chardges of o<sup>r</sup> howse at Bantam at the least.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">We wrote vuto yo<sup>u</sup> by Captaine David Middleton that yo<sup>u</sup> should noe more pay yo<sup>r</sup> selues yo<sup>r</sup> wages by Royalls of 8 at iiij<sup>s</sup> the peece but att such a moderate rate, as might be indifferent:<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-124" href="#sec-07-fn-124" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-124" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> yett now vpon better considera<span class="sc">c</span>õn the Company haue concluded, and giue yo<sup>u</sup> direct order to pay noe more wages at all there, then may be needfull for the comelie apprellinge of any of o<sup>r</sup> ffactors<sup>rs</sup> w<sup>ch</sup> we deeme to be x<sup>li</sup> or 20 m<sup>r</sup>ke at the most p̄ Annū and the rest to be paid here in England, either to be adventured in the generall stocke or to such of yo<sup>r</sup> freinde, as yo<sup>u</sup> shall assigne the Company to pay yt vnto. And w<sup>th</sup>all such wages as yo<sup>u</sup> shall pay to lett the Ryall of 8 for fyve shillinge.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Alsoe we signifie vnto yo<sup>u</sup> all, that althoughe any of yo<sup>u</sup> shall <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-406" data-page="406" data-folio="314" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n405_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 314">[p. 314]</button> succeede such as were before yo<sup>u</sup> in place yett there shall be noe succession of increase of wages allowed vnto yo<sup>u</sup> except yt be by spetiall order &amp; direc̃cõn from the Governo<sup>r</sup> and Company. Wee haue appointed S<sup>r</sup> Henry Middleton to be o<sup>r</sup> Generall, vnto whome we wish yo<sup>u</sup> to shewe yo<sup>r</sup> selues obedient, and to doe as he shall ymploy yo<sup>u</sup> whether yt be by tarieinge there, or goeinge to any other place whether he shall see occasion to carry yo<sup>u</sup>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Althoughe we had giuen order heretofore that noe man should bringe home any goode in o<sup>r</sup> Shipps of the nature and kinde that we o<sup>r</sup> selues doe lade, yett the greatest nomber in the Dragon brought home store of pepp, w<sup>ch</sup> causeth vs to thinke that the same was stolne from vs and occasioned the want we found of o<sup>r</sup> pepp in the said Shipp. ffor prevencõn whereof hereafter we haue reiterated the same Comandement, and wish eu<sup>r</sup>y one of yo<sup>u</sup> that shall either be at the cleiringe of any of o<sup>r</sup> Shipps away from thence, or come home in any of them, that yo<sup>u</sup> see and make diligent inquirie amongst the Company of the said Shipp publiquelie, whether any of them haue any goode of such kinde yea or noe. yf they haue and can make due proofe that they bought yt there, then yo<sup>u</sup> are to pay them for yt &amp; shoote yt into the hould: yf they haue none signifie soe much vnto vs, that they denied to haue any there.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">We haue alsoe taken order, that whosoeu<sup>r</sup> shall lade any goode in o<sup>r</sup> Shipps and shall not register the same w<sup>th</sup> the Pursar shall haue yt taken from him. And we requier eu<sup>r</sup>y one of yo<sup>u</sup> to doe yo<sup>r</sup> best that the same may be dulie pformed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">We will doe o<sup>r</sup> best endevo<sup>rs</sup> to prevent that none such as S<sup>r</sup> Edward Michelborne was, should disturbe yo<sup>r</sup> quiett whoe dyed a yeare since hopinge that tyme will weare out the remembrance of his deede.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">fforasmuch as we haue found greate inconvenyence by lettinge of yo<sup>r</sup> money there at 2 or 3 for one to be paid heare, whereby diu<sup>r</sup>s of the Mariners and their wyues and Children after their deathes haue beene ympou<sup>r</sup>ished we doe therefore thinke yt fitt, that noe such Exchandge be suffered or pmitted for the avoidinge of scandall both from yo<sup>u</sup> and vs.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It appeth by the Accompte sent home that yo<sup>u</sup> haue drawne yo<sup>r</sup> wages after iiij<sup>s</sup> the Ryall of 8 from the first of October 1605 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-407" data-page="407" data-folio="315" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n406_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 315">[p. 315]</button> to the 31<sup>th</sup> of October 1608, w<sup>ch</sup> being accompted after iiij<sup>s</sup> vj<sup>d</sup> the Ryall as yt costeth vs beside the adventure we finde you haue allowed yo<sup>r</sup> selues 470 Ryalls of 8 more then is yo<sup>r</sup> due w<sup>ch</sup> we expect to haue restored by yo<sup>u</sup> that haue receiued the same, as alsoe that from the 31<sup>th</sup> of Octoƀ 1608 yo<sup>u</sup> receiue the Ryall of 8 at 5<sup>s</sup> and not otherwyse.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thomas Harwood the Gunner of the Dragon giueth vs in accompt 20 barrells of powlder lefte w<sup>th</sup> yo<sup>u</sup> Iohn Hearne, whereof as of all thinge ells lefte vnder yo<sup>r</sup> chardge we expect a good accompt.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There is one W<sup>m</sup>: Lanwell lefte at Bantam whoe standeth indebted in the Pursars booke of the Dragon iij<sup>li</sup>—iiij<sup>s</sup>—x<sup>d</sup> w<sup>ch</sup> yo<sup>u</sup> are to defaulke out of his wages there and make the Company Creditor for the same.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">London the 15<sup>th</sup> of March 1609.</h3><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">To m<sup>r</sup> Hawkins and the rest of o<sup>r</sup> ffactors att Suratt &amp;c:</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Our</span> harty Comendačōns remembred &amp;c: yo<sup>r</sup> łres of the fourth of Octoƀ 1608<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-125" href="#sec-07-fn-125" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-125" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> we receiued p Moloneux m<sup>r</sup> of the Dragon whoe by Gode assistaunce aryued saffelie at Plymouth in Septemƀ last being 1609 subscribed by W<sup>m</sup>: Hawkins and W<sup>m</sup>: ffinche, together w<sup>th</sup> a note from W<sup>m</sup>: ffinche of wares aswell there to be bought as sould w<sup>th</sup> waighte measures valuačōns of moneys &amp; other pticular intelligences to good purpose and w<sup>ch</sup> we cannot but Comend wishinge the lyke carefullnes to be contynued in yo<sup>u</sup> all. Wee vnderstand of yo<sup>r</sup> saffe arriuall at the Barr of Suratt and of landinge yo<sup>r</sup> goode, together w<sup>th</sup> that vnhappie surprize of yo<sup>r</sup> men &amp; goode by the Portugalls,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-126" href="#sec-07-fn-126" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-126" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> we doe vnderstand alsoe of the season of the yeare and of the depthes of o<sup>r</sup> Shipps for that place, of all w<sup>ch</sup> aduise we doubt not but to make good vse hereafter. Yett we cannot but blame yo<sup>u</sup>, for sendinge so much goode and moneys a shore, before yo<sup>u</sup> had bought or laden any goode backe againe, and alsoe for adventuringe the ymploym<sup>t</sup> of all yo<sup>r</sup> moneys in one bote, yt <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-408" data-page="408" data-folio="316" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n407_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 316">[p. 316]</button> beinge the first that yo<sup>u</sup> sent from the shore to the Shipp, whereas yf you had sent lesse at once w<sup>th</sup> good caution, yo<sup>u</sup> might haue prevented o<sup>r</sup> losses of goode and men in greate measure yf not altogether, And howsoeu<sup>r</sup> yo<sup>u</sup> excuse yt, that yo<sup>r</sup> men were fainte hearted and yealded w<sup>th</sup>out resistaunce, yett we are informed by Bucke and Marlowe now prisoners in Lisbone that they were destitute of powder and other meanes to defend them selues, w<sup>ch</sup> was a greate neglect in yo<sup>u</sup> to be soe secure,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-127" href="#sec-07-fn-127" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-127" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> as not to arme and animate yo<sup>r</sup> men thorowlie, beinge in the Middest of dangers, and comittinge soe much goode to their chardge, we are informed by some of the Dragons Company that yo<sup>u</sup> had sould much of yo<sup>r</sup> lead and other english Comodities at Suratt, but in your łres yo<sup>u</sup> mention onlie the disbursem<sup>te</sup> of all yo<sup>r</sup> monie, yo<sup>u</sup> write vs alsoe of greate Elaphauntę teeth bought by the way but not what they cost, such thingę yo<sup>u</sup> doe not well to omytt we hope that o<sup>r</sup> twoe shipps that we sett out hence in April 1608 . the <em>Vnion</em> and the <em>Assencõn</em> haue visited yo<sup>u</sup> as we gaue them Comission, And that by makeinge saile of yo<sup>r</sup> english Comodities yo<sup>u</sup> did furnish yo<sup>r</sup> selues w<sup>th</sup> Indico &amp; other Cambaia goode in due tyme, yo<sup>u</sup> giue vs good incourradgm<sup>t</sup> touchinge the easinesse and the greate benefitt of placeinge a factory in Suratt and other places neare Cambaia, w<sup>ch</sup> we alwaies soe conceaued, &amp; cheeflie aymed at yt, to w<sup>ch</sup> end beside all other meanes wherew<sup>th</sup> we did furnishe yo<sup>u</sup> thether we gaue yo<sup>u</sup> direcčõns, łres, and presentę, wherew<sup>th</sup> soe soone as you should be landed to addresse yo<sup>r</sup> selues to visitt the greate Magoll, that by his protecčõn yo<sup>r</sup> residence and ffactory at Suratt, might be more safelie setled before any further violence or treacherie of the Portugalls plottinge there, might be put in execučõn against yo<sup>u</sup>, w<sup>ch</sup> we greatlie feare yo<sup>u</sup> did not hasten to prevent, till yt was too late, neither can we gather by yo<sup>r</sup> łres that yo<sup>u</sup> did land any of yo<sup>r</sup> presentę for the kinge, and therefore we cannot but feare that yo<sup>r</sup> interteynem<sup>t</sup> w<sup>th</sup> him will be accordinglie, and moreover in that spetiall other Cawtion w<sup>ch</sup> we gaue you to beware and not to trust the Portugalls, we finde to o<sup>r</sup> Coste that yo<sup>u</sup> were too carelesse, all w<sup>ch</sup> Considered thoughe we hope the best, yett we cannott but <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-409" data-page="409" data-folio="317" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n408_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 317">[p. 317]</button> rest very doubtfull both of yo<sup>r</sup> saffetie and of o<sup>r</sup> goode, till wee doe heare further, we haue furnished in theis shipps for Suratt Elaphaunte teeth, Quicksylver, and other Marchandize, for w<sup>ch</sup> yo<sup>u</sup> gaue vs aduise, and whereof the Cape Marchaunt M<sup>r</sup> ffemell hath the Invoices, w<sup>th</sup> the sales whereof, as alsoe with his direcc̃ons what to send ffor England, he is to make yo<sup>u</sup> acquainted, &amp; therein we requier yo<sup>u</sup> to assist him w<sup>th</sup>all dilligence, and w<sup>th</sup> yo<sup>r</sup> best endevo<sup>rs</sup>. Those base thinge as sweete fennell seede, Cum̃yn, longe pepp, and such lyke as are not worth the fraight, we doe not expect nor doe wishe yo<sup>u</sup> to send any: we haue resolued and doe thinke yt fitt M<sup>r</sup> Hawkins, that yo<sup>u</sup> doe in the first of theis three shipps retourne for England w<sup>th</sup> yo<sup>r</sup> accounte Iournall, and such other yo<sup>r</sup> obseruac̃ons touchinge the affaires of those Countries, that by yo<sup>r</sup> intelligence we may be the better directed in o<sup>r</sup> further proceedinge to settle in those parte, and in any hand we giue yo<sup>u</sup> straight chardge to foresee and prevent that the Mariners nor any other be suffered to pester the Shipps w<sup>th</sup> luggage, w<sup>ch</sup> abuse had well neare beene the losse of the Dragon in hir nowe comeinge home, beinge pestred for want of gou<sup>r</sup>nm<sup>t</sup> (eu<sup>r</sup>y man doeinge what he listed) w<sup>th</sup> at least one hundred Tuns of Mariners goode more then her burthen.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">And forasmuch as we haue heretofore found that the priuate trade of our ffacto<sup>rs</sup> hath beene carefully carryed for their owne Lucar and gaine w<sup>th</sup> the greate neglect of o<sup>rs</sup>, conuertinge all the ritche &amp; pretious thinge to their owne vse &amp; nothinge for the Company but pepp and grosse Comodities, and seeing theis three shipps in this Sixt voyadge, are the begynninge of a new accoumpte, we haue ordered that nowe and hencforth neither o<sup>r</sup> Generalls Marchante ffacto<sup>rs</sup>, nor any other that we ymploye shall be p̄mitted any pryvate dealeinge or trade for them selues and therefore S<sup>r</sup> Hen: Middleton himselfe, M<sup>r</sup> ffemale and all the rest that are now ymployed in this shippinge haue putt their moneys into the generall stocke, and haue giuen their seu<sup>r</sup>all Bonde w<sup>th</sup> suerties as them selues can witnesse not onelie to forbeare all priuate trade directlie or indirectlie for them selues, but alsoe to forbid and hinder all others from the lyke, and therefore soe many of yo<sup>u</sup> of the third voyadge as are now there resident, or shall be lefte there to Contynew still, notw<sup>th</sup>standinge any lib̄tie of priuate <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-410" data-page="410" data-folio="318" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n409_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 318">[p. 318]</button> trade much or little admitted vnto yo<sup>u</sup> in the said third voyadge &amp; seeinge this voyadge is the begynninge of a new account wherein we intend to carry an even and equall hand to all that we ymploy, we doe therefore lett yo<sup>u</sup> knowe that you are hencforth to forbeare all priuate tradinge for yo<sup>r</sup> selues, &amp; to ymploy yo<sup>r</sup> selues onlie and whollie for the generall stocke giueing bonde to S<sup>r</sup> Henry, M<sup>r</sup> Downton<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-128" href="#sec-07-fn-128" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-128" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> or M<sup>r</sup> ffemale to the Companies vse for the true pformance thereof, onlie for apparrell and such vses yo<sup>u</sup> are to take of yo<sup>r</sup> wages soe much as will suffice soe yt exceed not x<sup>li</sup> p̄ Annū accounteinge the Ryall of 8 at 5<sup>s</sup>, and the rest of yo<sup>r</sup> wages to be paid yo<sup>u</sup> in England, neyther hath the Generall or Cape Marchant any authoritie or power to augment wages w<sup>th</sup> any of yo<sup>u</sup> but we haue power to gratifie any man that we shall thinke to deserue the same, w<sup>ch</sup> may be sufficient incourradgm<sup>t</sup> to styrr vp your best endevo<sup>rs</sup> in o<sup>r</sup> seruice. And we haue giuen order to displace and bringe home all such as by their refusall shall shewe them selues to be otherwyse disposed then in o<sup>r</sup> Comission we haue ordayned. And towchinge S<sup>r</sup> Edward Michelborne (whoe is dead almost a yeare since) howsoeu<sup>r</sup> we suspected him to haue gotten his goode by wrongfull courses yet we knowe not in pticular whome he had wronged. Therefore as yett we can say nothinge to that matter in as much as we receiued not the Captaines łres w<sup>ch</sup> you mention were taken by the Portugalls, that he had directed to o<sup>r</sup> kinge complaineinge of his losses And thus wishinge yo<sup>r</sup> prosperyty and good successe we Comitt yo<sup>u</sup> to God and rest.</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-07-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">At a Court held on the 15th December, 1607, the governor and others “were “intreated to Consider what Comissions and lres aswell from his ma<sup>tie</sup>: as from the “Companie wilbe now convenient to be sent, And therevpon to cause the same, to be “drawne and made readie in due tyme before the departure of the shipps” (<em>Court Minutes</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>i.e.</em> page 103.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The last four words have been added by another hand.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 109.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 251, note 2.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 252, note 1.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently an error for “and.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">In the Company’s general instructions for the voyage (<em>see</em> page 245), orders were given for the despatch overland of messengers, for whose safety a royal safe conduct (no doubt the present document) had been procured; and in later letters the Company <span class="folio-turn">[p. 233]</span> expressed their strong disapproval that their orders on this point had not been carried out. It would appear that Jourdain and Glascock were the two factors designated for this duty.<br class="fn-para">John Jourdain or Jourdan was elected a factor on 7th Dec., 1607, and was assigned to the <em>Assention</em>. On the wreck of the vessel he appears to have made his way to Agra, and thence back to Surat, where in Oct., 1611, he embarked on Sir Henry Middleton’s ship, and accompanied him in his voyage to the Red Sea and afterwards to Bantam. He seems to have been actively employed during the next few years in trading voyages from Bantam to various parts of the East Indies, and in 1614 we find him “president of the English at Bantam.” He returned to England in 1617, but in the same year entered into an agreement with the Company to serve them for a fresh term of five years; and accordingly by Nov., 1618, he was back again in his old post as President at Bantam. Eight months later he was slain in a skirmish with the Dutch at Patani. His journal, covering the period between his departure from England in March, 1608, and his return in 1617, is now in the British Museum (<em>Sloane MSS.</em>, 858).<br class="fn-para">Philip Glascock was elected a factor at the same time as Jourdain, but a few days later was rejected in favour of Richard Wickham. On 22nd Jan., 1607/8, he was again elected, and proceeded on the voyage. Nothing of importance can be traced concerning his further career.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The manuscript is here imperfect. A large portion of the leaf has been torn away, causing considerable gaps both in this and the succeeding letter. These <em>lacunæ</em> have been indicated by the insertion of brackets.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The date is torn away, but it should evidently be some day in March, 1607/8, as appears from the following entries:—<br class="fn-para">Further m<sup>r</sup> Gou<sup>r</sup>no<sup>r</sup> was now by this Courte intreated to write his lre to m<sup>r</sup></span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Added by another hand.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Joseph Salbancke was possibly the original member of the Company of that name, who adventured 240<em>l.</em> in the <em>First Voyage</em>; and hence, it may be, the consideration with which he was now treated. After the wreck of the <em>Assention</em> he proceeded to Agra, and thence started with Covert through Persia and Arabia to make his way overland to England. At Bagdad, however, they met an Englishman named White, who informed them of Middleton’s expedition; whereupon Salbancke determined to make for the Red Sea, hoping to meet Middleton, and acquaint him with the particulars of Sharpie’s voyage and shipwreck. On his journey he heard a false story of Middleton’s capture, whereupon he tried to make his way to Socotra, but fell into the hands of the Portuguese, who carried him to Muscat, thence to Ormuz and Goa, and finally, it would seem, to Lisbon, where he was released and returned to England. In 1612 he went out again as cape merchant in the <em>Expedition</em> of the <em>Twelfth Voyage</em>. He was at home once more in 1614, but seems to have left again for the Indies in the following spring with Keeling on a voyage on account of the <em>First Joint Stock</em>. In [Nov.?] 1616 he writes from Agra, where he seems to have remained for some time. In 1618 he took part in a voyage from Surat to the Red Sea, when a firman was obtained from the Pasha of Sana for trade at Mocha, &amp;c. Nothing can be traced as to the time or place of his death, but it must have been before the end of 1624, as by Jan., 1625, his widow had married again.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">There are gaps here and in the next few lines, owing to a portion of the leaf having been torn away (<em>see</em> note, page 233).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Att this Courte was red a lre from the Adventurers in the 3<sup>th</sup> to their ffactors “at Bantam, or els where in the Indies, and allowed to be sent as it was now agreed “vpon. And further yt was ordered That amongst other thinges therein mencioned “yt be incerted into the said lre. That yf the Adventurers in the 4<sup>th</sup> voyadge shall “happen to leaue any ffactors in the Indi[es] where the ffactors of the 3 voyadge shall “be Resident, That the ffactors in both th[e] voyadges doe remaine and liue together “At the chardge equallie pro rato according to the quantitie of goodes w<sup>ch</sup> shall be “theare left for each accompt. And further yt was agreed. That yf the shipps of “the Adventurers in the said 4<sup>th</sup> voyadge shall happen to bringe home any of the “goodes of the Adventurers in the 3 voyage, they are to pay freight for the same “after the rate of [<em>figures deleted</em>] <sup>li</sup> per Tunn &amp;c: As by the same lre Coppied into “the Register at lardge Appereth. A Coppie of w<sup>ch</sup> lre was now alsoe deliured to m<sup>r</sup> “Sharpie Gen<sup>r</sup>all for better vnderstandinge what therein was to be performed on his “parte &amp;c.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 12th March, 1607/8.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is entered on a separate sheet of paper, which has been fastened into the book at this place. It is endorsed “The draught of the bill of Aduenture left by m<sup>r</sup> “Graeffe,” and appears to represent “m<sup>r</sup> Graeffe’s” idea of how his bill of adventure should be worded. The document actually adopted will be found at page 269.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Marginal note added by another hand.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Philip Grove, de Graeffe or Grave, a Fleming by birth, was second pilot in the <em>First Voyage</em>, master of the <em>Dragon</em> in the <em>Second</em>, and master of the <em>Assention</em> in the <em>Fourth</em>. It is not known what became of him after the shipwreck of the latter vessel.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Att this Courte were red and allowed the Comissions from the Company to” the said Alexander Sharpie &amp; Richard Rowles dated the last day of ffebruarie, and “the Blanckes in the same Comissions for the ffactors names vnfilled, but left to the” Gen<sup>r</sup>alls discrecons.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 5th March, 1607/8.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Died at Priaman about the end of 1609.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Reparted.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 136.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 118.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 232.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note, page 134.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This paragraph, as well as some of those that follow, is so evidently based upon the information given by Sir Edward Michelborne in his interview with the Company, that it has been thought well to give <em>in extenso</em> the interesting account entered on the Court Minutes of 26th Jan., 1607/8:— “Att this Courte S<sup>r</sup> Edward Michelborne as form<sup>r</sup>lie he had promysed appeareing, “declared that for the desier he had to ymparte his knowledge vnto them for the “Good of the Company, he was now come hither, whoe signiefied as followeth. “ffirst that by experience he had found that mens healthes were best p<sup>r</sup>served by “labor and refreshinge out of the shipps &amp; in the hotest places by plentie of meate “and drinke then espetiallie to be yealded them, keepeth their Bodies freest from “diseases. “Then that much perrill and small hope of trade may be expected at Aden, yt “being a Garrison Towne of Souldiers, rather then of Marchauntes, yett neare to “Aden aboute some 10 miles of, there is a Towne (as yt seemeth he is informed) “called Moccha, gou<sup>r</sup>ned w<sup>th</sup> m<sup>r</sup>chauntes onelie, and a place of spetiall trade right “ou<sup>r</sup> against w<sup>ch</sup> there is an Iland called Kedgee, wherein is good Harbor &amp; watering “yf need require. “Then that by noe meanes o<sup>r</sup> shipps and men can goe saffelie to Suratt, or theare “expect any quiett trade for the many dangers likelie to happen vnto them by the “Portugalls, Cheeff Comanders of <em>Diu</em> and <em>Demon</em> &amp; places there aboute very stronge “&amp; hath the sole Comand of those Seas, all the people theare paying them tribute &amp; “the kinge of Cambaia recydeth twoe monnethes iorney from Suratt &amp; the Barr “shallow. “But he saith there is a place not farr thence, twoe daies saileinge from Suratt, “called Lawrie [<em>see</em> p. 251] in the Bay of the River Syndus, wheare is good Harbor in “saffetie; Twoe miles from w<sup>ch</sup> vp in the Bay there is a greate Cittie like London called “Nigertuttie [<em>see</em> p. 252], where is plentie of Indico Callico &amp; diu<sup>r</sup>s m<sup>r</sup>chandize, the “people there are free onelie gou<sup>r</sup>ned by kinge Acabar whoe resideth at Agree some “ffortie miles from Nigertuttie at w<sup>ch</sup> the Portugalls haue nothing to doe but onelie “come thither for m<sup>r</sup>chandize as other Strangers, where he conceiueth yf o<sup>r</sup> People “doe goe thither behaueing them selues well they will find ample and proffitable “trade aswell of their Comodities as for vent of o<sup>rs</sup>, at w<sup>ch</sup> place Cloth is in greate “request, of the particulars whereof, he will be readie to informe m<sup>r</sup> Sharpie &amp; “m<sup>r</sup> Rowles more at lardge. “He alsoe declared that Iecketta is afitt place to right o<sup>r</sup> selues of such of those “people that should offer vs any wronge, the w<sup>ch</sup> place is scituate in the River Syndus “but for the fittest tyme of the yeare to that purpose, he is vnwillinge to declare.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 236.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 122, note.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Blank in MS.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Daman, in Gujarat, still (like Diu) in the possession of the Portuguese.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Lahori Bandar, on the south or left bank of the Baghiár or western branch of the Indus, 20 miles from the Piti mouth. Though formerly the principal port of <span class="folio-turn">[p. 252]</span> Sind, it has now fallen into complete decay, in consequence of the channel on which it is situated having ceased to be navigable. Hamilton in his <em>New Account of the East Indies</em> (1727) describes it as a village of about 100 houses, built of crooked sticks and mud, with a large stone fort mounting four or five guns. The harbour was accessible to ships of 200 tons burden, and a brisk trade was carried on.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Nagar Thato, the native name of Tatta, a town on the Indus in the Karachi district of Sind. It is now sadly shorn of its former glories. Hamilton, who visited it in 1699, speaks of it as “the emporium of the province, a very large and rich city;” and he estimates its dimensions at that time as about three miles long, and one and a half broad. The East India Company had for some time a factory there.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is the copyist’s improvement upon Sir Edward Michelborne’s “king Acabar” (see note, page 247).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 237.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink fn-unlinked">2 · [Unlinked note, p. 253]</strong> <span class="fn-body">Richard Reave was chosen factor, for his skill in trade and languages, on 22nd Jan., 1607/8. He was, with Captain Rowles and several others, treacherously slain by the natives in Madagascar on the outward voyage.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Or?</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 69.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably “here” is intended.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a blank space here in the MS.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">21?</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “into.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 128, note.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 129.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 129, note.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sizes.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 73, note 10.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note, page 134.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">One of the factors in the <em>Assention</em>. After the shipwreck, he set out with the others for Agra, but died upon the road.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 231.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 232.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 227.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 69.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 136.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See pages 73-86.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 234.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note, page 234.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 236.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 250.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 217.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Immediately after the ships of the <em>Fourth Voyage</em> had been despatched, preparations were made for the equipment of a <em>Fifth</em>. The <em>Merchant Royal</em> of Hull was mentioned at a court held on 22nd March, 1607/8, as a suitable vessel, and three members of the Company, including Mr. Humphrey Smyth, were requested to make the necessary enquiries. On the 19th April, it was agreed that Robert Earle should be sent to Hull to view the ship, and to “deale therein accordinge to o<sup>r</sup> instruccons “giuen him &amp; as he shall be directed by m<sup>r</sup> Humfrey Smyth vnto whome we purpose “to direct o<sup>r</sup> lres to that effect: as by o<sup>r</sup> Register (of such thinges) at lardge appereth.” On the 14th June, 15<em>l</em>. was voted to Mr. Earle for “his paines &amp; chardges” in going to Hull, but nothing is said as to the result of his mission. The ship was not bought, but this may have been due to the resolution come to that the <em>Fifth Voyage</em> should not be sent out by the adventurers in the <em>Fourth</em>, as had been intended, but by the adventurers of the <em>Third Voyage</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on the preceding document.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Riders are timbers reaching from the keelson to the orlop-beams, to bind the ship and give additional strength. When a rider is lengthened by means of pieces batted or scarphed to it, the latter are known as futtock-riders. (Smyth’s <em>Sailor’s Word Book.</em>)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Standards.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Att the suite of the wieffe of Phillipp de Graeffe . . . . for a Bill of Adventure “of the gaine of 550<sup>li</sup> accordinge to order the 18<sup>th</sup> of November last. Yt was agreed “that some fitt Bill w<sup>th</sup> the advice of m<sup>r</sup> Deputie be made and deliured her as shee “requireth . . . . . and that yt be noted vpon the same Bill of Adventure, what “moneys Graeffe hath allreadie receiued, &amp; howe much over and aboue his ymprest “&amp;c: for future memory thereof.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 14th June, 1608.) See also page 239.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Added in another hand.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>i.e.</em> the Court Minutes of that date.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>i.e.</em> the preceding document.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Was red a Bill of Adventure, to be made &amp; given vnder the Companies seale “to all &amp; eu<sup>r</sup>y the principall Adventurers in the 4<sup>th</sup> voyadge to theast Indies &amp; ordered “to be engrossed &amp; deliu<sup>r</sup>ed accordinglie.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 22nd Nov., 1608.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“It is further ordered That all those w<sup>ch</sup> haue paid to the Threr of the Company “m<sup>r</sup> Thomas ffarrington all such somes of money as they haue sett downe &amp; adventured “in the third voyadge to Theast Indies. Haue now yealded them by the Company “vnder their Comon seale a Bill of Adventure for the same. And for the Secretaries “more exact doinge thereof, by yealdinge eu<sup>r</sup>y man his due, m<sup>r</sup> Stevens the Accompt “Keeper is from tyme to tyme to giue him true notice what each man hath soe “Adventured, &amp; haue then alreadie paid in the same to o<sup>r</sup> Threr m<sup>r</sup> Thomas ffarrington “as aforesaid.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 16th Feb., 1607/8.)<br class="fn-para">“Att this Courte for the true makeinge of Bills of Adventure, according to what “each man hath adventured in the third voyadge to the East Indies yt was ordered “that the Bills be made as it was now agreed &amp; that all the said Adventurers, doe “deliu<sup>r</sup> vnto m<sup>r</sup> ffarrington Threr their acquittances taken of him, for such moneys “as they haue paid for settinge forth of the said voyadge to Conferr the same w<sup>th</sup> his “&amp; the Accompt Keeperes Bookes, that therevpon the Bills of Adventure may be made “accordinglie, in w<sup>ch</sup> m<sup>r</sup> Threr is from tyme to tyme to write before they be sealed “theis wordes viz: (Examined to agree w<sup>th</sup> the accomptes by me Thomas ffarrington “Threr) vpon sight whereof, the same are to be sealed by m<sup>r</sup> Gou<sup>r</sup>no<sup>r</sup> w<sup>th</sup> the Comon “seale of the Companie.” (<em>Ibid.</em>, 26th April, 1608.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">John Moore (see the ensuing list).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">William Leighton was Secretary to the Company in 1607, having been elected at some previous date not precisely known. The last entry relating to him is in 1609, but the loss of the fourth Court Book (1610-14) prevents us from fixing exactly the date when he ceased to hold this post.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably intended to mark a transfer of shares, the name of the person to whom they were transferred being left for after insertion.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The name “George” has been crossed out and “Elizabeth” substituted, doubtless as that of his widow and executrix. Mrs. Coles subsequently married Sir Robert Leigh, who was made free of the Company on 28th July, 1609 (<em>Court Minutes</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In place of “William ffysher,” deleted. William Fisher’s adventure was “set over” to John Holloway on the 31st Oct., 1608.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Replacing “Alphonsus ffowle,” the original owner of the share. The date of the transfer was 26th Dec., 1608 (<em>Court Minutes</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Added by another hand, no doubt as a distinguishing mark.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for 20,000<sup>li</sup>. The patent referred to will be found on page 224.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Royal Commission for the <em>Fifth Voyage</em> (see note, page 115). There is a minute of this commission among the Domestic State Papers in the Record Office.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">At a Court held 13th April, 1609, the Company’s letters to be sent to their factors at Bantam in this voyage were “openlie read Considered and allowed of.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not now extant. They are referred to as received in the Court Minutes of 19th September, 1608.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Read to the Court on 16th and 18th January, 1608/9, but now missing from the records.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 147.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">“<em>Item</em>, in the Moneths of February and March, heere [Bantam] commeth three” or foure Iunckes from <em>China</em>, very richly laden with Silkes raw and wrought, ..... “<em>viz.</em>, Raw-silke of <em>Lamking</em> [Nanking?], which is the best, an hundred and” ninetie Rialls the Peecull: Raw-silke of <em>Canton</em>, which is courser, eightie Rials the “Peecull: .....” (Saris’s notes printed in <em>Purchas</em>, Lib. IIII., ch. 2.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sukadana, on the west coast of Borneo.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Lahore.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Vansleb’s “Indigo named Serquis,” <em>i.e.</em> of Sarkhej, 5 miles S.W. from Ahmedabad. <em>Shirkhist</em> is the bazaar name for every kind of Manna imported into India; and is the original Persian name for “Khorassan Manna.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Vansleb’s “Indigo of Balludri” <em>i.e.</em> Valabhi [Βυζάντειον of <em>Ptolemy</em>, viii. 1, 7], commonly written Ballabi, now an obscure village about 20 miles N.W. from Bhavanagar, but formerly the seat of the famous Valabhi kings, who, in succession to the mysterious Guptas, ruled over Kathiawar <span class="sc">A.D.</span> 318 to 790. The Arab writers of the 9th century name the place Balhara, a dialectic corruption of <em>bara-rai</em>, and signifying (the City of) “the Great (Valabhi) King.” See E. Thomas in <em>Numismata Orientalia</em>, Vol. iii., 1882. But for its close association with “<em>Serchis</em>” I should have identified “<em>Belondri</em>” here not with Ballabi, but with Vansleb’s “Mirabolans surnamed Balludri”; <em>i.e. bahira</em> [Sanskrit and Hindi], <em>berda</em> [Mahrati], <em>bahadrha</em> [Telugu], <em>beleyluj</em> [Arabic], <em>beleyleh</em> [Persian], the dry astringent fruit, with its kernel, of <em>Terminalia Bellerica</em>, the “Belleric Myrobalan,” used in dyeing. The “Chebulic Myrobalan,” used in the same way, is the fruit of <em>T. Chebula</em>. Neither of these was the μυροβάλανος, or “unguent-nut,” of the ancients, which is usually identified with the <span class="folio-turn">[p. 288]</span> seeds of <em>Moringa pterygosperma</em>; but the acid fruit of <em>Phyllanthus Emblica</em>, used in India as a pickle, was their <em>Μυροβάλανος ἔμπλέτζ</em> [cp. Sanskrit <em>amlika</em>, and <em>amrita</em>, and Hindi <em>aonla</em>].</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A kind of gun or musket.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pistols.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Died at Bantam in 1610.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Left by Middleton at Bantam in 1610 as “chief in the house.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sophony (Sophonias) Cozuke (the Kazak, <em>i.e.</em> as he is also styled in the Court Minutes, “the Russe”), entered the Company’s service, perhaps under the patronage of Sir Thomas Smyth, in March, 1609, on an engagement to stay in the Indies seven years. In 1613 he established a factory at Sukadana, in Borneo, and in the following year attempted to do the same at Landak. Two years later, he took an active part in an expedition sent to establish a factory at Banda, in the Moluccas, but failed to effect his immediate purpose, owing to the opposition of the Dutch. In Oct., 1616, he sailed again, with Capt. Courthop, to make a fresh attempt, and this time the opposing parties came to actual blows. The <em>Swan</em> was assailed by a Dutch vessel and captured after a severe fight, in the course of which Sophony was “beaten in pieces with a great shot” (Jan., 1617).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Died at Bantam in 1610.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Blank in MS.; perhaps sandalwood, or “saunders” as it was also called, is intended.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Buy.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Than.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">A truce for twelve years had just been concluded between Spain and her revolted provinces.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is John Davis of Limehouse, who has been so persistently confused with his more famous namesake of Arctic fame. He had been in the <em>First Voyage</em> under Lancaster, in Sir Edward Michelborne’s expedition of 1604-6, and (first as pilot and afterwards as master) in the <em>Third Voyage</em> with Captain David Middleton. After serving in the <em>Fifth Voyage</em> (here referred to), he went out again in the <em>Ninth Voyage</em> (1612-15), and on the captain’s death succeeded to the command and brought the ship home. In 1617 we find him master of the <em>Swan</em>, which was captured by the Dutch off Banda, and the crew made prisoners. After his release, he was appointed pilot major of the <em>James</em>, which sailed in January, 1621. He was by this time advanced in years, and probably weakened in constitution by his intemperate living; and in December of the same year he died, while the <em>James</em> was on a voyage to Jambi in Sumatra. Davis, though an able and intelligent seaman (witness his “Ruter,” or sailing directions, printed in <em>Purchas</em>), was headstrong and quarrelsome, and much addicted to drink; and this perhaps accounts for the Company’s unwillingness (in spite of his experience) to employ him in posts of greater responsibility.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">To be dated about March or April, 1609. For a note on Edmond Scott see page 34.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the form of the “bill,” see page 273.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Richard Atkinson, “servant to the governor,” was chosen cashier for the <em>Fourth Voyage</em> on 28th July, 1607; and in January, 1608/9, having been “ever found verie “faithfull &amp; very fitt for those businesses,” was appointed general cashier to the Company. In March, 1614/5, some of the secretary’s work was transferred to Atkinson. In August, 1621, he had to submit to a reduction of salary from 150<em>l</em>. to 100<em>l</em>. He seems to have died in the early part of the following year, and was succeeded by William Hurt.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on page 275.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This Commission was read and approved at a Court held 13th April, 1609.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Than.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Some words appear to have been omitted here (cp. page 258).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note, page 130.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “or.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “or.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Thomas Clayborne was appointed master’s mate at 6<em>l.</em> per month on 13th March, 1609. It had been proposed to offer him the post of pilot, probably before it was known that Davis was willing to go to sea again. Clayborne had previously been with Sir Henry Middleton in the <em>Second Voyage</em>, of which he wrote the account given in <em>Purchas</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Elected in July, 1607, “husband” for the Company, a post which, with some slight interruptions, he held for very many years.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“And further yt is ordered that twoe lres be written in the name of the Companie. “The one . . . . . Thother to m<sup>r</sup> W<sup>m</sup>: Kealeinge, m<sup>r</sup> W<sup>m</sup>: Hawkins, &amp; to “all other the Companies Officers &amp; servauntes in any their shipps comeinge out of “the East Indies in the behalfe of the Officers of his Ma<sup>ties</sup>: Customes, &amp; for reserue- “inge of all strange fowles &amp; beastes &amp;c: for the kinge and Councell, of w<sup>ch</sup> Tenor “ffower lres were written as appereth in the Register &amp; deliu<sup>r</sup>ed to m<sup>r</sup> Worstenholme “to be sent and disposed of as should be thought fitt.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 14th Aug., 1609.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 219. This letter was read and committed to the charge of Mr. Eldred, “whoe is intreated &amp; hath taken vpon him to doe the same &amp; to furnish them w<sup>th</sup> “moneys accordinge to the Tenor of the same lres,” at a Court Meeting on 14th Aug., 1609.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 164, note 6.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Itt is ordered that a lre be writt to the Iustices at Crayford to deale w<sup>th</sup> the “twoe Carpenters gone away, accordinge to the statute in that case prouided, as by “Register of Lres at lardge appereth.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 25th September, 1609.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">“And further yt is ordered That a lre be writt to S<sup>r</sup> Robert Mansfeild for leaue “to lay the Iron Ordinance on the kinges Wharffe at Debtford as at lardge in the “Register Appeareth.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 25th September, 1609.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The arrival of the <em>Dragon</em> at Plymouth (in the <em>Third Voyage</em>) was announced at the Court held 25th September, 1609, but she was in so bad a state that it was the 15th of November before her arrival in the Downs was reported; “wherevpon yt was “ordered that m<sup>r</sup> Barber doe w<sup>th</sup>all expedicon retourne againe to the Dragon w<sup>th</sup> “Comission to foresee and prevent that noe kinde of Commodities be Conveyed out “of her &amp;c: as by the same Comission appereth.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>.)</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A letter to the factors of the <em>Fourth Voyage</em>, sent by the ships of the <em>Sixth Voyage</em>, under Sir Henry Middleton.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 245.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Said to have been the largest merchant ship up to that time built in England.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 46, note 3.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Proposed for “general” in the <em>Fourth Voyage</em>, but “spared” until the next expedition. On 13th Nov., 1609, he was appointed a factor for the <em>Sixth Voyage</em> at a salary of 200<em>l</em>. per annum, with the stipulation that he should remain in the Indies at least three years. He died suddenly, 29th May, 1611, at Mocha, of poison, administered, as was thought, by the Aga, whom he had threatened to report at Constantinople.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-121" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-121" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A corresponding letter to the Commanders of the <em>Fourth Voyage</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>i.e.</em> twenty hundredweight.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-123" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-123" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See pages 236 and 285.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-124" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-124" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Fair to both sides, just; cp. the prayer in <em>English Church Liturgy</em> that those in authority “may truly and <em>indifferently minister justice</em>.”</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-125" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-125" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not now extant.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-126" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-126" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 219, note.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-127" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-127" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Unsuspecting. It was in his “secure hour” that Hamlet’s father was murdered.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-128" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-128" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Nicholas Downton was the lieutenant general of the <em>Sixth Voyage</em>, and had charge of the <em>Peppercorn</em>, with which he returned in safety, though in sore plight, in the autumn of 1613. In the following spring he sailed again, in the <em>New Year’s Gift</em>, in command of a voyage on account of the <em>First Joint Stock</em>. At Surat he had to sustain a formidable attack from a Portuguese fleet, but successfully repulsed them. He next proceeded to Bantam, which was reached in June, 1615; and here he died on the 6th August, “death lying in ambush to intrap,” says Purchas, “whom by “open force he could not deuoure.”</span></li></ol></section>
